(needs_source) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:05:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:06:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:06:30}
- The speaker names the six as Umar, Uthman (the transcript's "Uthman ibn Uthman" is read as Uthman ibn Affan), [[person/zubayr-ibn-al-awwam|al-Zubayr]], [[person/talha-ibn-ubaydullah|Talha]], [[person/sad-ibn-abi-waqqas|Sa'd ibn Abi Waqqas]] and [[person/ali-ibn-abi-talib|Ali]], with [[person/abu-ubayda-ibn-al-jarrah|Abu Ubayda ibn al-Jarrah]] as a seventh. He says that as the conquests spread and armies were sent to Iraq and the Levant, large armies needed men of weight and standing to lead them. He gives Abu Ubayda for the Levant and Sa'd ibn Abi Waqqas for Iraq; the line on where al-Zubayr was needed is garbled and not written. The speaker adds that three of them went out and three stayed with Abu Bakr, among them Umar, Uthman and Ali (Talha is not named in the passage). {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:07:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:07:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:08:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:10:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:10:30} (needs_source)
- He says Umar stayed beside Abu Bakr as an adviser, not for the battles, and was called the keeper of Abu Bakr's secret (the sentence breaks off at the name; the subject is Umar). {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:10:30} (needs_source)
- A first stand of Uthman under Abu Bakr, as the speaker tells it. A severe drought came because of the continuing wars (the transcript is unclear on one further difficulty), and Abu Bakr prayed, in paraphrase, that God not let them pass the night without relief. The speaker says that before sunset a caravan of Uthman's arrived at Medina with food, a thousand pack camels by his word and figure (the transcript first reads the word as "rihla", then "rahila"), and that the dust of the caravan covered Medina. He recalls that in the last episode Uthman had equipped a third of the army of hardship (the line is partly unclear) and still had a thousand camels, and he says that the more Uthman's wealth grew the more he gave. (status: tradition; needs_source) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:11:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:12:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:12:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:13:00}
- The merchants of Medina came and knocked at his house; he came out in a wrap (the transcript gives an unclear reason) and asked what they wanted. They said the drought had struck and they had come to buy from him in order to feed the poor, and he told them to come in. Asked what they would pay for his goods, they said ten for twelve; he said others had offered him more; they said ten for fourteen, and he said the same; then ten for fifteen (the transcript then falls into a repetition, discarded, and the figures after it are unclear). They said they could not go higher, it was too much for them; he said he had offered it to someone who would pay him more than they would (the wording is unclear), and told them to rise: the thousand camels were charity for the poor. The figures are the speaker's. (status: tradition; needs_source) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:13:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:14:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:14:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:15:00}
- The speaker says this was Uthman in Abu Bakr's caliphate, and moves to the day Abu Bakr was dying, when he called Uthman to write what he would dictate. He digresses that Uthman was one of the scribes of revelation: when revelation came to the Prophet, the Prophet's leg or foot (the transcript's word is unclear) rested on Uthman's as he wrote, and the Prophet told him to write. The speaker has Aisha say, in his rendering, how dear to God must be a man for whom revelation came down to a prophet while he wrote it; the rest of the sentence is garbled. (status: tradition; needs_source) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:15:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:16:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:16:30}
- Abu Bakr began to dictate and asked Uthman what he thought of Umar, saying he was thinking of appointing him. The speaker notes that Uthman was about sixty and that Umar's caliphate would last ten years, so that Uthman was seventy when he took office, and says that at sixty he might have hoped for it himself. Uthman's answer, as the transcript gives it, is that Umar is a man whose private conduct is better than his public conduct and that he knows none better. Abu Bakr then consulted a group one by one (the transcript does not make clear who) and all said Umar. The speaker says he is concentrating on Uthman's stand. (needs_source) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:16:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:17:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:17:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:18:00}
- As the speaker tells it, Abu Bakr dictated the opening of the testament, that this was what Abu Bakr ibn Abi Quhafa bequeathed as he left the world, and had begun "I have appointed over you" when he was overcome and fainted. Uthman wrote Umar ibn al-Khattab, though Abu Bakr had not said it, out of fear that Abu Bakr would die and discord follow (the transcript's wording of Uthman's fear is unclear). After a while, when Abu Bakr asked what he had written, Uthman read it, and Abu Bakr said, in paraphrase, "God is greatest", that he saw Uthman had feared he would die in a time of discord, and that God reward him well for his service to Islam. (The transcript's account of how Abu Bakr came round is garbled.) (status: tradition; needs_source) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:18:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:19:00}
- Under Umar, the speaker says, the two closest advisers throughout the ten years were Uthman and Ali (the line is partly garbled). He says Umar was stabbed in Dhu al-Hijja of 23 AH, the date being the speaker's, and turns to the last moments and the succession. {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:19:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:20:00} (needs_source for the date)
- When stabbed, Umar said he would not appoint a successor. They asked whether he would leave the people without a caliph; he said, in the speaker's rendering, that he would not bear it living and dead. They urged him to appoint someone to avoid discord, and he said that if [[person/abu-ubayda-ibn-al-jarrah|Abu Ubayda ibn al-Jarrah]] were alive he would appoint him, because he had heard the Messenger of God say, in paraphrase, that Abu Ubayda was the trustworthy one of this community. The speaker comments that Umar took a saying of the Prophet and built a great matter on it (his reading, in a partly unclear line). Umar then said the same of Salim (the transcript reads "Salim, mawla of Abu Ubayda" and later "of Abu Fudayl", both garbled, and the museum's Salim mawla Abi Hudhayfa is not asserted here, so no link), on a saying, in paraphrase, that Salim greatly loved God. (status: tradition; needs_source) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:20:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:20:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:21:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:21:30}
- Both being dead, Umar said, in the speaker's rendering, that he would not appoint but would leave six. The six the speaker names are Uthman, Ali, [[person/abd-al-rahman-ibn-awf|Abd al-Rahman ibn Awf]], Talha ibn Ubaydullah, al-Zubayr ibn al-Awwam and Sa'd ibn Abi Waqqas (the speaker puts the question of the sixth to the studio and answers Sa'd; the transcript does not record the participants' replies). He says Umar then laid down rules for the choice, and wonders that such a method comes from fourteen hundred years ago. {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:21:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:22:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:22:30} (needs_source) (see also [[event/shura-council-of-uthman|the shura council of Uthman]], the museum's own record)
- The speaker's account of the arrangements Umar set. The six were to meet and shut themselves in a house, that of Aisha, after asking her leave, and consult for three days. [[person/abdullah-ibn-umar|Abdullah ibn Umar]] was to attend and consult with them; [[person/suhayb-ibn-sinan|Suhayb]] (the transcript's spelling varies) was to lead the people in prayer for those days; and Abu Talha al-Ansari was to station fifty men of the Ansar (the transcript once says fifty-one) around the house, the speaker requiring that they be of the Ansar alone. {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:22:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:23:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:23:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:24:00} (needs_source)
- The rules for a vote, as the speaker gives them in Umar's name. If five agree on one man and one refuses, the one who refuses is to have his neck struck; if four agree and two refuse, the two. If three agree on one and three on another, Abdullah ibn Umar is to judge between them; if the other side will not accept his judgment, they are to go with the side of Abd al-Rahman ibn Awf; and if they still will not accept, the three who refuse are to be killed. If the three days pass without a choice, Abu Talha is to tell them to choose now or he will kill them (the speaker repeats these rules at 00:25:30 and 00:26:00). The speaker notes that Umar set this out while dying and had not set it down before. The wording and the penalties are the speaker's rendering. (needs_source) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:24:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:24:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:25:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:25:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:26:00}
- He explains why six. Of the ten given the good news of Paradise, Abu Bakr had died, Umar was dying, and Abu Ubayda had died in the plague (the speaker's statement); [[person/said-ibn-zayd|Said ibn Zayd]] was left, and the speaker renders Umar as saying that his sister's husband should not enter, so the six remained. He gives a second reason: after the farewell sermon, he says, the Prophet looked at the people and said, in paraphrase, that he was pleased with Abu Bakr, Umar, Uthman, Ali, al-Zubayr and others (the end of the list is garbled), and Umar, bleeding, remembered it. The speaker comments on how Umar lived with the sayings of the Prophet. (status: tradition; needs_source) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:27:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:27:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:28:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:28:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:29:00}
- On why Suhayb led the prayer: the speaker says that if one of the six led it, people would say he was their imam, and that Suhayb was from outside the Arabs, so no one could take offence at the choice (the transcript shows an exchange with a studio participant that it does not separate). {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:29:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:29:30} (needs_source)
- On why the Ansar guarded the house: he says the six were all of the Muhajirun, and that the Ansar had made a pact with the Prophet to protect him and carry the religion, asking only for Paradise. He renders the Prophet as saying they should protect him as they protect their children and women, and, asked what they would have, answering "Paradise" and falling silent. The speaker adds that the Ansar are not found among the disputants, and that a guard drawn from the Muhajirun might lean to one of the six, so the guard was made up of the Ansar (the next sentence, on army commanders, is garbled and not written). {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:30:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:30:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:31:00} (needs_source)
- On the fallback to Abd al-Rahman ibn Awf, the speaker praises him as a very God-fearing man, a merchant and a wise one (the sentence after that is unclear). In the speaker's rendering Umar said he saw the matter as lying between two men, Uthman or Ali (the transcript's verb, "err", is unclear); when it was put to him that he should choose Ali, he repeated that he would not bear it living and dead and left it to the six, over none of whom, he said, the people differ (the lines are partly garbled). {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:31:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:31:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:32:00} (needs_source)
- He says the limit was three days because more could bring discord. In the speaker's rendering Umar then called the six, and said to Ali that if the people pledged to him he should not put the Banu Hashim on people's necks, and to Uthman that if they pledged to him he should not put the Banu Umayya on people's necks, lest a band come and kill him in his house. The speaker comments on how far Umar saw. (status: tradition; needs_source) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:32:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:33:00}
- The six met in the house of [[person/aisha|Aisha]]. The speaker says it was the Prophet's house, so they would be at their most God-fearing there, and that they asked her leave and consulted for three days. He remarks that the idea that women had no part in the religion is mistaken, and points to Aisha's place here (the transcript's second clause, on Uthman's view of women, is garbled). {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:33:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:34:00} (needs_source)
- Those inside, as the speaker names them, were Uthman, Ali, al-Zubayr, Sa'd and Abd al-Rahman, with Abdullah ibn Umar consulting. The transcript's name for the sixth, who did not attend, is garbled at its start, with "ibn Ubaydullah" legible at its end; the museum reads it as Talha ibn Ubaydullah because the other five are named, and the speaker's later "and Talha of course" fits. The speaker gives a reason for his absence tied to Medina at the time of Umar's death (the transcript's wording is unclear) and says he did not know of the meeting. {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:34:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:35:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:36:30} (needs_source)
- The talk began in silence, the matter being heavy. Abd al-Rahman asked which of them would be content to leave the matter, and then said he himself would; when the rest were silent he asked again, then put it to Sa'd ibn Abi Waqqas, who said yes; the transcript then has "I and my cousin are out of this matter" without making clear who says it (the speaker afterwards counts Abd al-Rahman and Sa'd as out). The speaker says three were left, Uthman, Ali and al-Zubayr, adding "and Talha of course". Abd al-Rahman asked the three to agree that he would choose, on the condition that he ask and consult; they said yes, and he asked them to leave. {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:35:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:35:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:36:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:36:30} (needs_source)
- He then called each in turn and asked whom he would choose if he himself did not pledge and left the choice to the one asked. Ali answered Uthman. Sa'd, asked the same, answered Uthman. For Uthman's own answer the transcript falls into a repetition (discarded) and the line that follows is garbled, so it is not written. {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:36:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:37:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:37:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:38:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:38:30} (needs_source)
- Abd al-Rahman then went round Medina for three days, house to house, saying, as the speaker renders it, that he did not sleep for three days. The speaker says he asked everyone, even old women, children and young girls, whom they chose, and that he took a young nephew with him who kept count of whether people named Ali or Uthman. The speaker says Abd al-Rahman had taken himself out so that no one could have any suspicion about him, and says of Muslims that he is proud of Islam and of the Companions as models (his view). {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:39:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:39:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:40:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:40:30} (needs_source)
- Abu Talha with his men guarded the house, and the speaker renders his oath, by the One who took Umar's soul, that if they did not choose in three days he would deal with them (the verb is unclear and the end of the oath is lost). Meanwhile Suhayb led the people in prayer, and the people were at ease while the six deliberated. {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:41:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:41:30} (needs_source)
- On the last night, he says, most people had leaned to Uthman; the speaker suggests this is because people tend to prefer the elder in such a position. Abd al-Rahman sent the boy to fetch Ali and Uthman. The boy, in the speaker's retelling, asked which to start with and was told whichever he liked, and since his own inclination was with Ali he went to him first. Ali asked who else had been sent for, and which of them he had been told to start with; on hearing the answers he came, and they found Abd al-Rahman at night prayer. In the boy's retelling Ali said to him "your heart is with me" and Abd al-Rahman later said "your heart is with Ali"; the rest of Abd al-Rahman's words to the boy are partly unclear. (status: tradition; needs_source) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:41:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:42:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:42:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:43:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:44:00}
- He says that when Abd al-Rahman felt them come he shortened his prayer and finished it, and the speaker reads this as a matter of priorities: the night prayer is dear, but the question of the community's government came first. {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:44:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:45:00} (speaker's view)
- Abd al-Rahman asked Ali, and then Uthman, separately, whether he would pledge on the Book of God, the sunna of His Messenger and the way Abu Bakr and Umar acted. Ali answered, in paraphrase, no, but to the limit of his effort and capacity; Uthman answered yes. The speaker comments on Ali's answer (the comment is partly garbled); his remarks on each man's character are garbled and not written. Abd al-Rahman thanked each and sent him out. (status: tradition; needs_source) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:45:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:45:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:46:00}
- He went out wearing the turban which, the speaker says, the Messenger of God had wound on him, and said "come with me to the mosque". The caller called "prayer in congregation" in the last part of the night, and the mosque filled. The speaker says Abd al-Rahman went up on the Prophet's pulpit, on the step where the Prophet had been (the transcript uses both "sat" and "stood"), wearing the turban mentioned and a sword (the transcript says "his sword" without making clear whose), and adds that no one had used that step since the Prophet's death (the line is partly garbled). Abu Talha stood with his fifty. (the transcript's line on where Abu Bakr and Umar stood is partly garbled) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:46:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:47:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:48:00} (needs_source)
- Before everyone, Abd al-Rahman called Ali and put the same question, and Ali gave the same answer; then he called Uthman, who the speaker says came bashfully, and put it again, and Uthman said yes. {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:48:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:48:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:49:00} (needs_source)
- Abd al-Rahman raised his hands and prayed at length, and the speaker says the words are not known. He then took Uthman's hand, looked at the ceiling of the mosque and said, in the speaker's rendering, "O God, hear and bear witness" twice, that he was putting what was on his neck on the neck of Uthman ibn Affan, and he pledged to him. The speaker says Ali pushed through the rows to pledge, so that Ali's was the second hand, and that the people then crowded to pledge. He reports that they say no pledge in Medina was ever easier, simpler, greater or more complete than this (he does not say who). (status: tradition; needs_source) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:49:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:49:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:50:00}
- The speaker says Uthman stood on the Prophet's pulpit bashfully, at about seventy, and that the transcript's account of where he then sat is garbled; it reads as though he sat on a lower step than the Prophet's. His first words, which the speaker shows on the screen, are given in paraphrase: that he had been burdened and had accepted; that he was a follower and not an innovator; that the people had a claim on him, after the Book of God and the sunna of His Prophet, that he follow those before him in what they had agreed, and the way of Abu Bakr and Umar; and that the world is green and made attractive, so they should not incline to it nor be tempted by it, for it is not to be trusted and does not leave anyone but those who leave it. (needs_source: the speech as the speaker presents it) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:50:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:50:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:51:00}
- He says that Uthman had not prepared to become caliph, and that the pledge to him was on a Saturday in 24 AH, the transcript's wording of the day and month ("wahid muhkam") being garbled; the museum reads it as the first of Muharram, which is unchecked. He closes the story of Uthman's succession and says the next episode will begin Uthman's caliphate, and ends with the closing prayer and greeting. (the figures and the date are the speaker's) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:51:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:51:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep14 00:52:00} (needs_source)

## Left out of this episode 14

Not written from episode 14: the speaker's analogies and appeals to listeners and company owners about the value of a religious person in a street, building or company (00:07:30 to 00:09:30), his address to the wealthy at 00:15:30, his aside on the beauty of Islamic history and the Companions as models at 00:39:30 beyond the one line above, and his personal remark on what Paradise would be at 00:44:30. Garbled lines are not written: the comparison of Uthman with another figure (00:05:00), where al-Zubayr was needed (00:08:30 to 00:09:00), the father's first words in the boy's story (00:03:30 to 00:04:00), the end of the list of those with whom the Prophet was pleased (00:28:30), the lines on army commanders and the Ansar (00:30:00 to 00:31:00), Umar's prayer at 00:33:00, Uthman's own answer to Abd al-Rahman (00:38:00 to 00:39:00), the speaker's arithmetic of Ali's age (00:37:30), the lines on where Abu Bakr and Umar stood and where Uthman sat (00:47:30 to 00:50:00) and the lines at 00:51:30 on who was present as Uthman spoke. The studio participants' answers to the speaker's questions (00:20:30, 00:22:00, 00:29:30) are not recorded in the transcript. Qur'an verses are not involved and the sayings of the Prophet and the speeches are paraphrased, not reproduced. The repetition loops at 00:11:00, 00:14:30, 00:26:30, 00:38:00, 00:43:30 and 00:47:00 are not speech and are discarded. The ages, dates and figures in this episode are the speaker's: Uthman's age at accession, the thousand camels and the bargaining figures, the dates 23 AH and 24 AH, and the numbers of guards.

## Episode 15: Uthman ibn Affan's first letters as caliph and the conquests of his reign: the eastern front, Ifriqiya, the first fleet, Cyprus and Dhat al-Sawari

:::perspectives
- **Speaker's view (opinion):** He reads the order of the first four caliphs as fitted to each stage of the community: Abu Bakr was the one closest in manner to the Prophet, so that the community could bear the shock of his death gradually; Umar was firm and centralising, for the founding of a state and the conquests; Uthman was gentle and tender with people, and the speaker says that with him intellectual and civilisational creativity and the conquests surged forward. This is his reading, not a sourced judgement. (needs_source) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:04:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:04:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:05:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:05:30}
- **Speaker's view (opinion):** He argues that the conquests brought freedom and security to the peoples they reached, and that Islam was not spread by the sword. As he puts it, the armies first told the people of a land to worship as they wished, and the Persian and Roman rulers had been keeping their peoples from hearing Islam, so the armies asked to be left to present it to them. His account of the offer made to the rulers is partly garbled in the transcript. He adds that today satellite channels and the internet make it possible to present Islam to anyone without war. These are his arguments, given without sources in the recording. (needs_source) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:11:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:11:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:12:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:12:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:13:30}
- **Speaker's view (opinion):** He says that Uthman's letter to the army commanders shows a strong, not a weak, personality, in a man he gives as seventy years old, and he sets this against what he says will be said of Uthman in the next episode (that line is partly garbled). (needs_source) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:21:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:21:30}
- **Remarks the transcript reads as a studio participant's (opinion):** After the speaker asks whether anyone wishes to comment, the transcript carries remarks that are not in the speaker's own manner. The transcript does not name the participant or reliably separate the voices, so this is the museum's reading. One remark takes up Uthman's first letter: the world is to be taken out of the heart but not out of the hand, so that each Muslim learns, studies and becomes distinguished and a model in his place, and need not give a large sum but can give a little and be confident and positive. A later line says the Companions' battle plans were ingenious and begins a question that the transcript does not carry through. (needs_source) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:49:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:49:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:50:00}
:::

- The speaker opens a new episode and recalls the course of the series: the martyrs of Uhud and many Companions whom people hardly know by name, then Abu Bakr, then Umar, then Uthman ibn Affan. He says his wish is that listeners feel a deep love for Uthman, whom he calls the great Companion that the Prophet married to his daughter. He says, as a report he does not source, that Uthman was the fourth to enter the religion, "a quarter of Islam" (the line before it is partly garbled; it includes the phrase "fourth of four in Islam"). {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:00:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:00:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:01:00} (needs_source: the claim that he was the fourth is the speaker's)
- The speaker gives words as Uthman's own, in his rendering: that a day came when he was a quarter of Islam; that the Messenger of God married him to his daughter and, when she died, to his other daughter; that, in the words "if I had a hundred I would marry them to you, Uthman", a hundred were to be married to him (the transcript does not make clear who says it); that he never fornicated and never drank wine, in the Jahiliyya or in Islam; and that he never went out to the Friday prayer without freeing a slave, and if he found none to free he added the slave to the next Friday's, so that he freed two. (the transcript's verb for freeing is garbled and the sense is taken from the line that follows; the name the people gave him is garbled and not written; a further oath about his right hand is garbled and not written) (needs_source: the speech is given in the speaker's rendering) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:01:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:02:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:02:30}
- He recalls that the previous episode told how Uthman became caliph, the way the caliph was chosen, the Companions' piety, and [[person/ali-ibn-abi-talib|Ali]] pledging to him, and says he will now set out, on the screen, the state of the community on the day Uthman took office. {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:05:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:06:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:06:30}
- First on his list: the complete end of the state of the Persians. He says a state of seven centuries was ended in ten or fifteen years, so that there was nothing left called Persia. These are the speaker's figures. {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:06:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:07:00} (needs_source: the figures are the speaker's; a following line names a state that is garbled and is not written)
- Second: the end of the authority of "al-qawm" in the East; the transcript's word is garbled and the museum reads it as the Romans, since the line goes on to say they left Syria, Damascus, Palestine and Egypt and went back across the sea. He says the East was left with nothing but Islam. {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:07:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:07:30} (needs_source: the speaker's account; the reading of the word is the museum's)
- Third: that the Muslims carried the banner of freedom to the earth. He says that before the Muslims, the peoples suffered the tyranny of the Persians and of a second power that the transcript names in a garbled form (read by the museum as the Romans), with heavy taxes and slave-like conditions, and that Umar came and then Uthman took over the earth (the lines after that on its condition are garbled). {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:07:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:08:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:08:30} (needs_source)
- To illustrate, he retells a story he says he told before. A Copt living in Egypt raced the son of the governor of Egypt, whom the transcript names "Umar ibn al-As" and the museum reads as [[person/amr-ibn-al-as|Amr ibn al-As]]; the son grew angry and struck him, saying "I am the son of the noble" (who won the race is not clear in the transcript). The Copt travelled to Medina to [[person/umar-ibn-al-khattab|Umar]], who summoned the governor and his son and told the Copt to strike the son of the noble, and he did. The Copt then said it was only the son who had struck him, and Umar answered that the son struck him with the authority of his father; then Umar turned to the governor and said the sentence the speaker stresses: since when have you enslaved people when their mothers bore them free (the start of the sentence is garbled in the transcript and this wording is the museum's reading). The speaker says this sentence is said today by "al-umma al-muttahida" (the transcript's words, read by the museum as the United Nations); his line about some Muslims and the West is garbled. (the transcript's line about whom Umar told the Copt to strike next is garbled; the account is the speaker's, not checked, and the governor's son is not named) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:08:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:09:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:09:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:10:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:10:30} (needs_source: the story and the claim about the United Nations are the speaker's)
- He tells his listeners that what he is describing will, in the next episode, bear on why Uthman was killed, and that the earth changed from one state to another in ten years (the lines are partly garbled). {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:10:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:11:00}
- Fourth, on freedom of belief, he says that when the Muslims entered a country they told its people to go about the streets and markets and worship whatever they wished, citing the Qur'anic principle that there is no compulsion in religion (the verse is not written here). He gives two reasons that the armies went out: that the peoples were free to keep their religion, and that the Persian and Roman rulers prevented their peoples from hearing Islam, so the Muslims asked them to leave the people to hear it. He says God commanded the Muslims to present the religion to the whole earth, to be believed or not, and imagines how things would be had the first Muslims not carried Islam to Muscat, Iraq, Azerbaijan or India. The lines that follow on the jizya are partly garbled and are not written. (the speaker's argument is not sourced in the recording) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:11:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:11:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:12:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:12:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:13:00} (needs_source)
- He says today satellite channels and the internet allow Islam to be presented to anyone in the world, so that the idea reaches minds even without war, and that Islam is not eager to fight and kill. He adds that people of other religions were secure on earth only in the shade of Islamic civilisation, and gives as an example the departure of Muslims from al-Andalus, after which, he says, the Jews went looking for where the Muslims were going, because they felt safest under Islam (the lines on the Maghrib and the Portuguese are garbled). These are the speaker's remarks. {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:13:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:14:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:14:30} (speaker's view; needs_source)
- Fifth, a united and cohesive community, without tribal zeal or anyone trying to split it; sixth, an Islamic administrative system: he credits Umar (the transcript's form of the name is garbled) with founding the diwans, registers for the people and for the soldiers, says an earlier episode was given to it, and speaks of a state with its foundations and ministries; seventh, a community that loves victory and has very high morale. He asks how Uthman will move and whether he will continue the conquests, and goes to a break. {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:15:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:15:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:16:00} (needs_source: the speaker's list)
- He says the first thing Uthman did on taking the caliphate was to issue three letters. The first, to the whole community, surprised people, he says, because a rich man was expected to incline to the world. In the speaker's rendering it said, in substance: you are in a place of passing and in the remainder of your lives, so hasten to do what good you can; the world is folded on deception, so let neither it nor the deceiver deceive you about God; take lessons from the past, strive and do not be heedless, for you are not neglected; where are the sons of the world and its brothers, who preferred it, built it and enjoyed it long, and did it not cast them out; throw the world where God threw it and seek the next life. The letter then continues with Qur'anic verses on the likeness of the life of this world, which the speaker recites and which are not written here. He imagines the rich Uthman speaking first of the world. (the letter is given in the speaker's rendering and in paraphrase, without a chain in the recording) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:16:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:17:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:17:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:18:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:18:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:19:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:19:30} (status: tradition; needs_source)
- The second letter, he says, went to the commanders of the armies in the lands, to turn attention to the conquests. In paraphrase: you are the protectors of the Muslims; Umar laid down for you a course that is not hidden from us and I was present and heard what Umar said to you, so let no change or substitution by any of you reach me, or God will change what is in you and replace you with others; see how you are, for I am looking into what God has made me responsible for. The speaker warns his hearers not to change what is between them and God, and says the worst thing is for God to be pleased with a person and then replace him. (the letter is in the speaker's paraphrase; needs a source) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:19:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:20:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:20:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:21:00} (status: tradition; needs_source)
- The third letter, to the governors in the far lands: he says that he will take the governors to account at every hajj season; that he ordered them to command good and forbid evil; and that no complaint against any governor would reach him without the complainant getting his right (the line on his own and his family's rights is garbled and not written). The speaker adds that Uthman set a rule that anyone with a grievance in his own land should come to him at the hajj, and that the caliph would pay the costs of that person's hajj, and he mentions three days whose sense in the transcript is not clear. He calls these Uthman's first three acts. (status: tradition; needs_source) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:21:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:22:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:22:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:23:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:23:30}
- The speaker says the achievements of the reign were enormous, that the trouble came only in the last two years, which the next episode will tell, and that the preceding years were of extraordinary achievements; the conquests, he says, did not stop until the unrest began. (a figure of fifteen years in the line before is unclear and is not written) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:23:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:24:00} (needs_source: the speaker's periodisation)
- He sets out three fronts: eastern, western and a third that the transcript calls "al-wusta al-bahriyya" (the maritime one, as the rest of the episode on the fleet shows), and says the term was heard for the first time in Islam after the Prophet's death. On the eastern front, he says, Uthman concentrated forces at two bases, Kufa and Basra: six thousand fighters at Kufa and four thousand at Basra (the speaker's figures). The Kufa force, under [[person/said-ibn-al-as|Said ibn al-As]], whom he calls a Companion, went to three places; the transcript's name for the first is garbled; the second is al-Rayy (the transcript's "al-Ghay", read as al-Rayy), which he says was the capital and Tehran a suburb of it, and that after a fire there Tehran grew in its place (the speaker's statement); and Azerbaijan. {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:24:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:25:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:25:30} (needs_source: the figures and the geography are the speaker's)
- The speaker voices a request to the caliph: give me the fewest troops, but give me these six, naming al-Hasan and al-Husayn ([[person/hasan-ibn-ali|Hasan]] and [[person/husayn-ibn-ali|Husayn]]), [[person/abdullah-ibn-abbas|Abdullah ibn Abbas]], [[person/abdullah-ibn-masud|Abdullah ibn Masud]], [[person/abdullah-ibn-umar|Abdullah ibn Umar]] and "Abdullah ibn Umar ibn al-As" as the transcript writes it (the museum reads [[person/abdullah-ibn-amr-ibn-al-as|Abdullah ibn Amr ibn al-As]]). The transcript does not make the requester's name clear; the lines that follow read as the caliph telling Said ibn al-As to take them and go with six thousand (partly garbled). The speaker's link of the names to the request is his account and is not checked. {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:26:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:28:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:29:00} (needs_source)
- Of the result he says nothing stood before the army and the people of those lands [a verb the transcript garbles] before it; that it conquered al-Rayy, Azerbaijan and Armenia in one year, "year 26"; and that the earth had become theirs within twenty-six years (the line that fixes the starting point is garbled). These are the speaker's dates and figures and are not checked. {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:29:00} (needs_source: the year and the figure are the speaker's)
- The Basra force, he says, went toward Khurasan, and he names as its commander al-Walid, whom the transcript calls "al-Walid ibn Uqba ibn Abi Muadh" (the museum reads [[person/al-walid-ibn-uqba|al-Walid ibn Uqba]]), and gives Said ibn al-As as the commander on the Kufa side. He then recalls that Uqba ibn Abi Muayt ([[person/uqba-ibn-abi-muayt|Uqba ibn Abi Muayt]]) was the man who harmed the Prophet while he prostrated (the object he threw is garbled), and says Uqba was the husband of Uthman's mother, and that his son al-Walid nevertheless became a commander of the Muslim forces. He says this shows the refinement of Islam (the lines before are garbled), and gives [[person/ikrima-ibn-abi-jahl|Ikrima ibn Abi Jahl]] coming to the Muslims at the conquest of Mecca, when, in the speaker's rendering, the Prophet told the Companions not to curse his father, because cursing the dead does not reach the dead and harms the living. He says Islam wants no harsh religion and that we love people and take them by the hand. He sums up the eastern front as the stretch from Tehran, Azerbaijan and Armenia to Afghanistan, under al-Walid and Said ibn al-As. (the speaker's assignment of commands is unchecked and the name forms are the transcript's) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:29:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:30:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:30:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:31:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:31:30} (needs_source)
- On the western front, Egypt and Syria. He says Syria's governor was [[person/muawiya-ibn-abi-sufyan|Muawiya ibn Abi Sufyan]] and that Egypt's governor under Umar, and in Uthman's first year, was a man whom the transcript names "Umar ibn al-As" (read by the museum as Amr ibn al-As, as above); then Uthman took the governorship from him and gave it to [[person/abdullah-ibn-sad-ibn-abi-sarh|Abdullah ibn Sad ibn Abi Sarh]] (the transcript spells the name several different ways). He calls this a long story and one of the problems that the next episode will take up. {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:32:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:32:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:33:00} (needs_source)
- In Syria, he says, a commander whose name is garbled in the transcript set out for "Amuriyya", south of Ankara, and by the year 28 the Muslims had reached Ankara, deep in the land of their opponents (the speaker's statements; the commander is not named here). {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:33:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:33:30} (needs_source)
- In Egypt, Abdullah ibn Sad began to conquer "Ifriqiya", which the speaker says was then Tunisia. He says the Romans gathered an army of twenty-one thousand and Abdullah had twenty thousand, and that Abdullah asked Uthman for men from among the Prophet's Companions; the speaker names those sent as Hasan, Husayn, [[person/abdullah-ibn-al-zubayr|Abdullah ibn al-Zubayr]] and Abu Dharr (the transcript's second name for Abu Dharr is garbled and not written), with the caliph saying he was sending those who would save them. The speaker reflects that the matter is not a question of numbers but of the meaning in the men. (the figures are the speaker's, unchecked) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:33:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:34:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:34:30} (needs_source)
- In his account the battle went badly and the Romans began to surround the Muslims. Abdullah ibn al-Zubayr offered to end it, asking for thirty horsemen to protect his back, and said he would kill the enemy commander (the commander is not named in the transcript). The speaker says he set off with the thirty behind him, cut off the commander's head and set it on a spear point, and that the enemy then scattered; he gives their number at that point as a hundred and twenty thousand, which does not agree with the twenty-one thousand given earlier. He says the battle ended and Ifriqiya was conquered. (the account is the speaker's; the lines in between are partly garbled and the figures are not checked) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:35:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:36:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:36:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:37:00} (needs_source)
- After it, he says, Abdullah ibn Sad began to be present in the Maghrib and a new commander appeared, [[person/uqba-ibn-nafi|Uqba ibn Nafi]], whose name, he says, listeners will hear again. After a break he recaps the eastern and western fronts and says Abdullah ibn Sad's name is a dangerous one and one of the causes (the words that follow are garbled) in Uthman's affair, and that many people have a problem with him; yet in the speaker's words, if he had a problem earlier and there was doubt about his Islam, he afterwards fought hard and supported the religion strongly, and he defers the story of the next battle to the next episode. {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:37:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:38:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:38:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:39:00} (needs_source: the speaker's account and defence)
- The speaker turns to the fleet. He says Muawiya, governor of Syria, wanted to do something new for Islam, to be the first to build a fleet to fight in God's cause and cross the sea, and wrote to Umar, who refused. As the speaker gives it, Muawiya argued that the other side was so near that from Hims one could hear dogs barking and hens crowing (the transcript does not say who is meant; the lines are partly garbled). Umar sent for a man whose name the transcript garbles (the museum reads it as Amr ibn al-As) to describe the sea (the transcript's word for it is garbled), and he is made to say, in the speaker's rendering, that it is a great thing in which the rider is very small, that its riders are like worms on a log, that it shakes the hearts and makes minds fly, and that doubt strikes even the firm believer in it. Umar then swore he would not carry a Muslim on the sea, saying that a single Muslim's safety mattered more; Muawiya fell silent. (the sayings are in the speaker's rendering) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:39:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:40:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:40:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:41:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:41:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:42:00} (status: tradition; needs_source)
- When Uthman became caliph, he says, Muawiya came to him and said they were being threatened by sea (the transcript does not say by whom). Uthman answered, in the speaker's paraphrase, go and trust in God, but compel no one; whoever wishes to go with you goes by choice. The speaker says this was the first fleet Uthman founded, and names Muawiya as one of the two who set it up (the transcript gives the second name as "Abdullah ibn Thabit" followed by an unclear word; the museum does not identify him). {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:42:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:42:30} (needs_source)
- The fleet moved first against Cyprus in year 28, as he gives it; the museum's record [[event/first-raid-on-cyprus|The First Raid on Cyprus]] gives the same year and Muawiya as its leader. The speaker says one fleet set out from Egypt under Abdullah ibn Sad and one from Syria under Muawiya, with [[person/ubada-ibn-al-samit|Ubada ibn al-Samit]] (the transcript writes "Ubad ibn al-Samit") and his wife, whom the transcript first names "Umm Hayran bint Milhan" and then Umm Haram ([[person/umm-haram-bint-milhan|Umm Haram bint Milhan]]). (the year and the commanders are the speaker's) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:42:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:43:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:43:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:44:00} (needs_source)
- He recalls, in paraphrase, that the Prophet once slept at her house, woke laughing and said that people of his community would cross this sea in God's cause, like kings on thrones; she asked him to pray that she be among them and he said she was among the first. The speaker says she sailed with her husband, and that when she landed in Cyprus she fell and died, in his words the first woman martyr in Europe and the first woman to ride the sea (the lines are partly garbled), and that her grave there is still visited. (the transcript's words for how she fell are garbled; his appeal to listeners about visiting the grave is not written) (status: tradition; needs_source) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:44:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:44:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:45:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:45:30}
- Then, he says, came the battle of Dhat al-Sawari ([[event/dhat-al-sawari-31|The Battle of Dhat al-Sawari (31 AH)]] in the museum; the speaker gives no year), the first naval battle of the Muslims. He says the Byzantine commander, Constantine son of Heraclius, prepared five hundred ships and Abdullah ibn Sad sailed with a fleet of what the transcript renders as "min matn markab" and the museum reads as two hundred ships. In his account the Byzantines offered the Muslims a fight on land or on the sea; Abdullah's reply is partly garbled. The speaker says the Muslims, to take control of the fight, lashed ships together (the lines on whose ships and on the wind are garbled), that the fighting was very hard and bodies came to the shore, and that Abdullah called on his men to recite the Qur'an, be patient and remember God. He says the Muslims won a decisive victory with remembrance and Qur'an on their lips. (the figures are the speaker's and the Byzantine commander's name is as the transcript has it) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:45:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:46:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:46:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:47:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:47:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:49:00} (needs_source)
- At about 00:48:00 the speaker breaks off with a story from Abdullah ibn Sad's earlier life, which he says he will tell next time; the lines are garbled and the story is not written. {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:47:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:48:00}
- He closes by saying these were the conquests in the reign of Uthman, and, on the several occasions he mentions it, that the next episode turns to the last two years and the killing of Uthman. He ends with the closing greeting. (needs_source: the speaker's outline of the next episode) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:49:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep15 00:51:00}

## Left out of this episode 15

Not written from episode 15: the speaker's contemporary analogies and appeals, among them his comparisons with night-prayer in a province or a building and his remarks on the clothes in people's wardrobes (00:01:00 to 00:03:30), his remarks on religious young people, on the headscarf as a beginning and on sport and the arts (00:27:00 to 00:28:30), his exhortation to pray in the mosque, rise at night and take part in religious work (00:34:30 to 00:35:30), a garbled aside on a modern state (00:33:00), and his appeal to listeners about the grave of Umm Haram (00:45:00). Garbled lines are not written: the lines on the number of soldiers and the order of campaigns (00:32:00), the place named at 00:25:00, the jizya (00:13:00), what he says of the Maghrib and the Portuguese (00:14:30), the second name given to Abu Dharr in the reinforcement (00:34:30), the commander of the Syrian advance (00:33:00), the identity of the second founder of the fleet (00:42:30), Abdullah ibn Sad's reply to the Byzantines and the lines on whose ships and on the wind (00:46:30 to 00:47:30), and the story of Abdullah ibn Sad's pledge (00:47:30 to 00:48:00). The studio participants' later remarks at 00:50:00 are cut off. The repetition loops at 00:03:30, 00:26:00, 00:40:00, 00:48:30 and 00:50:30 are not speech and are discarded. Qur'an verses recited in the letter and at the battle are not written, and the Prophet's sayings and the letters are paraphrased. The dates, numbers and names of commanders in this episode are the speaker's.

## Episode 16: Uthman ibn Affan's achievements, and how the fitna began and the accusations against him as the speaker answers them

:::perspectives
- **Speaker's view (opinion):** He holds that the community's troubles in Uthman's last years came less from Uthman's personality than from changes in the community itself after ten years: an enormous expansion and the fall of the Persian state within twenty-odd years (he says 27 or 28), wealth and ease reaching the Arabs on a scale they had not known, large numbers of peoples of different languages and ideas entering Islam, many of them still ignorant of the religion, some of them, he says, carrying a grudge against Islam and entering to harm it from within, and a wide freedom of expression in lands whose peoples had not had it (the word is garbled and is read as freedom from the sense of the line). These are his arguments, given without sources in the recording. (needs_source) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:23:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:23:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:24:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:24:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:25:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:25:30}
- **Speaker's view (opinion):** He reads the fitna as a trial God sends the community from time to time, after the lands had been opened to it, to show who holds firm to the religion, and he recites Qur'anic verses on being tried (the verses are not written and he does not name them in the transcript); he names the fitna in Uthman's time and the one in the time of Ali. (needs_source) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:26:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:26:30}
- **Speaker's view (opinion):** He says that people have tried for fourteen hundred years to destroy the religion (the line is partly garbled), that the first blow was the killing of Umar and the second a fitna set in motion by one hidden man, and that many of the rumours against Uthman are still believed by Muslims today. (needs_source) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:33:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:33:30}
- **Speaker's view (opinion):** He says the world has not known economic prosperity, religious tolerance, security and ease as it did in the period when the Muslims led it, and that people of other religions worshipped as they wished under Muslim leadership (his remarks are general, not about Uthman's time alone). The lines are partly garbled and these are his remarks, not sourced. (needs_source) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:13:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:14:00}
- **Speaker's view (opinion):** On the dispute over completing the prayer at Mina he says it is a difference over a branch of the law on which two views are possible, and that the worst fitna is for Muslims to hate and fight one another over such a difference; a person may take either view without the other having to be wrong. (needs_source) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:51:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:52:00}
- **Speaker's view (opinion):** He argues that Uthman was a strong, not a weak, personality: that a man who opened the East and the West, founded the first fleet, gathered the Mushafs at an age he gives as eighty, dismissed and appointed governors and ruled the community for ten years cannot be called weak. He says he loves Uthman and hopes his listeners will love and feel attached to him more. He gives his age as eighty here where episode 15 has seventy; neither figure is checked. (needs_source) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:55:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:55:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:56:00}
- **Remarks the transcript reads as studio participants' (opinion):** After the speaker asks who wishes to comment, the transcript carries several short remarks that are not in his own manner. It does not name the participants or reliably separate the voices, so this is the museum's reading. One says God may have had a wisdom in the fitna, to establish proof against Muslims until today, and that the Companions were human and not angels. One says he has heard that a Jewish man was at the start of the fitna and does not remember the name. One points to Noah, Ad and Thamud and to the fitna after the Prophet's death as trials of the Muslims, citing a Qur'anic verse that is not written, so that those who know the Companions' history do not turn away. One says the expansion meant that many did not know Uthman or his personality, and one that the fitna of money, when wealth increased, may have been one of the causes. Parts of these lines are garbled. (needs_source) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:19:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:20:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:20:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:21:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:21:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:22:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:22:30}
:::

- The speaker opens by recalling that the series began with Uthman ibn Affan, and says that today is the fifth episode on him (as the transcript has it). He says he will take up Uthman's achievements in the twelve years of his caliphate: that Uthman took office at the end of Dhu al-Hijja after Umar and stayed twelve years, a first part which he describes twice as ten years (the lines are garbled and repeat the figure), and that the last two years, when the fitna began, he will tell in detail today. {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:00:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:00:30}
- Recalling the conquests, he divides them into an eastern, a western and a sea front. On the eastern front he says the fighters of Kufa and Basra together were 10,000, though the "real total" was 40,000 (a line about each man going out once in four years is garbled and its sense is not written). The armies from Kufa, he says, reached what the transcript writes "Ghay" (read, as in episode 15, as al-Rayy, which he links with present-day Tehran in a garbled line), Azerbaijan and "Almaniya" (the form in the transcript; the museum does not identify it), and he says they reached the borders of Russia or the Russian republics of the Soviet Union. The army from Basra reached Khurasan. He remarks how far this was from Mecca. These are the speaker's statements and are not checked. {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:01:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:01:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:02:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:02:30} (needs_source: figures and places are the speaker's; the identification of the places is the museum's reading where marked)
- On the western front he says the armies went out from Egypt, and from a place the transcript writes "Taghdhulus" in Libya (garbled; not identified), and reached "Africa, which is Tunisia today", beginning to enter the lands of the Maghrib. The armies of Syria, he says, reached "Umuriyya" (the form in the transcript; episode 15 reads it as Amuriyya), south of present-day Ankara; he gives the year as 27 or 28 and marks the distance covered by recalling that 27 years earlier the Prophet had left Mecca for Medina with Abu Bakr alone. {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:02:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:03:00} (needs_source: the years and the places are the speaker's)
- The third front, he says, was the sea: the Muslims had not known sea warfare before Uthman (the word is garbled and is read from the context of the fleet), and he names the conquest of Cyprus ([[event/first-raid-on-cyprus|The First Raid on Cyprus]]) and the battle of Dhat al-Sawari ([[event/dhat-al-sawari-31|The Battle of Dhat al-Sawari (31 AH)]]; the speaker gives no year here) as coming under him, and says he told the battle in detail in the previous episode. {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:03:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:03:30} (needs_source)
- Of Dhat al-Sawari he says again that it was a very hard battle, that the Muslims moved with 200 ships (he marvels that so large a fleet was built in a few years) against the former great state, whom the transcript calls "al-Rum" (the Romans), who moved with 500 ships, and that after a long, hard fight the commander, whom the transcript writes "Abdullah ibn Sad ibn Abi Sufyan" (the museum reads Abdullah ibn Sad ibn Abi Sarh, with no link here as the reading is not certain), called on the Muslims to recite the Qur'an and remember God, and the battle ended in a Muslim victory. (A line about the opponents' state of mind before the battle is garbled and not written.) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:03:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:04:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:04:30} (the account and the figures are the speaker's)
- Among Uthman's other achievements, the speaker says he added greatly to the Sacred Mosque, and enlarged the Prophet's Mosque to what he gives as 160 metres in length and 150 in width, with a roof and columns of carved stone and six doors, the work beginning in 29 AH and ending in 30 AH (as the transcript has it; the figures are the speaker's and are not checked). He says Uthman also made the hajj season a meeting with all his governors, at which anyone who had been wronged could come and take his right, and a yearly conference at which he asked them for the news of their lands in detail, and he calls it a great thing for a state. {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:04:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:05:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:05:30} (needs_source: the speaker's account)
- He turns to the gathering of the Mushaf, which he says came in two stages. In the first, he says that at the Prophet's death many Companions had memorised the Qur'an and many had written parts of it, but nobody had it all in one place; Umar advised Abu Bakr to collect it, and Abu Bakr first asked how he could do what the Messenger of God had not done; Umar pointed to the death of many memorisers in the battle that the transcript writes "al-Yamna" (the museum reads the Battle of Yamama, with no link here as the reading is not certain). The man chosen was [[person/zayd-ibn-thabit|Zayd ibn Thabit]], a young man and one of the writers of revelation, who, in the speaker's rendering, said that being asked to carry a mountain would be easier for him; he went to one person after another and asked them to recite the surah, hearing it from one and then from another (the lines on two witnesses and two written copies are partly garbled and not written), and the speaker praises this as a scientific method. The collected pages were kept with [[person/hafsa|Hafsa bint Umar]] (the transcript's form of her name is garbled at first and the speaker then says "bint Umar ibn al-Khattab"), which he takes as a sign of the Companions' regard for women. (the story is the speaker's, not checked; status: tradition) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:05:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:06:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:06:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:07:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:07:30} (needs_source)
- In the second stage, under Uthman, he says the conquests had widened and the dialects multiplied; [[person/hudhayfa-ibn-al-yaman|Hudhayfa ibn al-Yaman]], who was on the campaign that the transcript calls "Almaniya", saw people disputing over how a verse was read. As the speaker has it from al-Bukhari, Uthman sent to Hafsa for the Mushaf she held and called four young Companions; the transcript names Zayd ibn Thabit, [[person/abdullah-ibn-al-zubayr|Abdullah ibn al-Zubayr]] and [[person/said-ibn-al-as|Said ibn al-As]], and the fourth is not clear in it. They were to copy it, and where they differed to write in the dialect of Quraysh, the dialect in which the Qur'an came down. Seven copies were made and one was sent to each of Syria, Egypt, Kufa, Basra, Yemen, Mecca and Medina (the transcript's form for Kufa is garbled and read so from the list). He says it is called "Uthman's Mushaf" because Uthman ordered it, while Zayd did the writing, and that Uthman ordered any other copy that a Companion held to be burned (the transcript's verb is garbled at 00:09:30 and 00:10:00; the sense is taken from the charge "burner of the Mushafs" at 00:44:00), so that the one copy relied on should be the one that spread. (status: tradition; needs_source: the speaker cites al-Bukhari but does not give a reference) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:07:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:08:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:08:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:09:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:09:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:10:00}
- Another achievement, he says, was the increase in stipends and gifts. The conquests brought spoils, and the fall of Persia and the lessened need for military spending left more money (the lines on this are partly garbled). Each Muslim had a sum from the treasury monthly; Umar had begun this, and Uthman, in the speaker's words, multiplied it many times over. He says Uthman gave every Muslim a dirham a day in Ramadan (a line on the Mothers of the Believers is garbled and not written), and set up beside the Prophet's Mosque something (the noun is garbled) from which anyone in Medina, however many, ate every day of the year. He quotes [[person/al-hasan-al-basri|al-Hasan al-Basri]] as saying he heard with his own ears a crier in the streets of Medina calling people to take their allowances, their clothes, and ghee and honey from the treasury. (the account is the speaker's; the saying of al-Hasan al-Basri is as the speaker gives it and is not sourced) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:10:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:10:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:11:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:11:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:12:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:12:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:13:00} (needs_source)
- He says Uthman also founded the first fleet of the Muslims, and he tells of Jeddah: that the port of Mecca had before been at a place the transcript calls "Shuayba", and that Uthman, who in his words had wide experience, judged that a better place existed, travelled to [[place/jeddah|Jeddah]] himself, went into its sea and said it was blessed. The speaker takes this as a sign of Uthman's far sight. (the story is the speaker's; the lines are partly garbled; needs_source) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:14:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:15:00}
- Then, he says, notable Companions began to die in Uthman's reign, since many had been 30 to 40 years old when the Prophet died. He names [[person/abd-al-rahman-ibn-awf|Abd al-Rahman ibn Awf]], who he says died in 32 AH and who had helped in the choice of the successor (a sum he gives after it is garbled and not written); [[person/abbas-ibn-abd-al-muttalib|al-Abbas]], the Prophet's uncle; [[person/abu-sufyan-ibn-harb|Abu Sufyan ibn Harb]], who, he says, died blind, having lost one eye at the battle of [[event/battle-of-hunayn|Hunayn]] (as the transcript has it) and the other at [[event/battle-of-yarmuk|Yarmuk]] (the year he gives is garbled); and [[person/abdullah-ibn-masud|Abdullah ibn Masud]]. Other names he gives are garbled and are not written. He says this brings the episode to the last two years, the hardest, the years of fitna. {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:15:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:16:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:16:30} (needs_source: the speaker's list and dates)
- After a break he says that, ten years into this stability, something strange began: a fitna that ended in Uthman's killing, which he leaves for the next episode, and that spread to Kufa, Basra and Egypt (the transcript's "Misr", read as Egypt), with rumours going out everywhere and heavy accusations against Uthman, and some governors whom people did not want and whom Uthman was asked to dismiss. He lists the talk as it was said of him: that he favoured his relatives (the transcript's word is garbled here; the same charge is stated clearly at 00:44:30), gave them from the treasury, was wrong in this or that, and went against the Prophet's Sunna. He asks why a settled community fell into fitna so suddenly, whether Uthman was the cause, and whether his gentle personality, set against Umar's firm one, explains it; he adds that some say Uthman was weak, and asks the people in the studio for comments. {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:17:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:17:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:18:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:18:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:19:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:19:30} (needs_source)
- Going on, he says the fitna began with a man named [[person/abdullah-ibn-saba|Abdullah ibn Saba]], a Yemeni from Sana'a whom he describes as a Jew who entered Islam outwardly to harm it, and skilled at deceit. He says he went to Medina first, then Syria, Basra, Kufa and Egypt, and compares the community to a victorious soldier with wounds and boils that need time to heal, which, in his picture, this man pressed on. To the objection that one man could not turn a whole community, he answers that he was not alone, giving as evidence that, as the transcript has it, wherever there was a battle between the Muslims and a side the transcript names in a garbled form, Ibn Saba was present, so that at Dhat al-Sawari he was on the Syrian border and seems to have been carrying news to that side, and that Ibn Saba, who was not known to have money, became rich after he entered Islam and spent on his followers; the speaker asks where the money came from. These are the speaker's arguments and the museum has not checked them against the historical sources. (The remarks at 00:29:30 to 00:30:30 on the people he links with the fitna and on a present-day dispute are not written.) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:27:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:27:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:28:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:28:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:29:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:29:30} (needs_source: the speaker's account)
- He says the man chose hypocrisy, since facing this religion openly would not have worked, and recalls that the opening of Surah al-Baqara sorts people into believers, disbelievers and hypocrites, with, in his counts, five verses on the believers, three on the disbelievers and a whole page on the hypocrites, because the one who enters the religion and shows religiousness while striking Islam from inside is the most dangerous. (the counts are the speaker's) {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:30:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:31:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:31:30} (needs_source)
- Of his course he says he tried Medina first and could not make the fitna take hold there; then Syria, where, he says, [[person/muawiya-ibn-abi-sufyan|Muawiya ibn Abi Sufyan]] was a strong, unusual personality who held the whole country; then Kufa and Basra, where he found people who were frightened or did not understand, and his followers were of several kinds: some who understood that he was fighting Islam, some who were angry at their governor only (the transcript's word is garbled; read as the governor) and wished to get rid of him, and some who understood nothing and repeated what they heard. He then moved to Egypt and settled there, where the line that follows is garbled. Rumours began to spread about Uthman. {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:31:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:32:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:32:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:33:00} (needs_source: the speaker's account)
- After a break he sets out to answer the accusations one by one; the lines about letters sent from city to city in the names of the Companions are garbled (not written). The first accusation, he says, was that Uthman struck [[person/ammar-ibn-yasir|Ammar ibn Yasir]] until his intestines burst (the transcript first writes "Umara ibn Yasir"); that the aim of Abdullah ibn Saba in it, he says, was to stir the feelings of ordinary Muslims by saying that a Companion had been struck. He says every slander has a root, and that the root here was a quarrel between Ammar and another Companion whose name is garbled and not written, which grew; that Uthman, whom he calls a strong personality, struck both to stop them from fighting each other (the lines on how, and a remark said to Uthman about the act, are garbled and not written); and that from this the rumour spread. He adds that when the fitna grew serious Uthman sent Ammar to Egypt to quiet it (the line is partly garbled), and that in Uthman's last hours, thirsty and asking for water (the words on why are garbled), Ammar went out in the streets of Medina calling on the Muslims to fear God and saying that the man who had bought something (the word is garbled) from which they were fed and given drink should not be left thirsty, then carried water on his shoulder into the midst of the rebels (the line that ends it is garbled). {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:34:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:34:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:35:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:35:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:36:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:36:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:37:00} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:37:30} {cite:assabile-amr-khaled wa-nal9a-al-aheba/ep16 00:38:00} (the speaker's account, not checked; needs_source)
- The second accusation: that he beat [[person/abdullah-ibn-masud|Abdullah ibn Masud]] until his ribs broke. The speaker calls this wrong and gives its root: Ibn Masud had a Mushaf of his own with notes written in its margins; when Uthman ordered the Mushafs to be gathered (the transcript's verb is garbled), Ibn Masud held on to his and Uthman insisted; Ibn Masud grew angry and asked who said his Mushaf was not right, and was told that it was [[person/zayd-ibn-thabit|Zayd ibn Thabit]], who had collected the Qur'an in Abu Bakr's time.
